People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

shift I come home and clean, rewrite notes, sort supplies, and pace around the kitchen while my husband is begging me to sit down.\n- key: S037\n  name: Pressured_Speech\n  description: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: When I start explaining the documentation problem I can hear myself talking faster and faster, but I can't stop because if I pause I feel like I'll lose the whole thread.\n- key: S038\n  name: Racing_Thoughts\n  description: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.\n  manifestation: My mind keeps jumping from wound measurements to patient initials to dates on the forms to what it all means, like ten tabs are open at once and none of them will close.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Requirement 1\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the manic episode\n  manifestation: I never heard the call-light voices or saw hidden messages in the charts before Like, I’ll hear it coming through the call-light speaker or from the vents in the hallway, and it’s clear—like someone standing right next to me telling me the records were altered. Even when it’s quiet and nobody else reacts to it.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever tell you to do something or take a specific action?\n\nPatient: Not really telling me to do something, no. It sounds clear, like someone standing right next to me, even when the unit is quiet and nobody else seems to hear it.\n\nDoctor: What kind of things do the voices say to you through the speaker and vents?\n\nPatient: They say which patient records have been changed. Like, I’ll hear it coming through the call-light speaker or from the vents in the hallway, and it’s clear—like someone standing right next to me telling me the records were altered. Even when it’s quiet and nobody else reacts to it.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever tell you to do something or take a specific action?\n\nPatient: Not really telling me to do something, no. It’s more like they’re telling me which patient records have been changed. I hear it through the call-light speaker or the vents, clear like someone is right there, but other people don’t seem to hear it.\n\nDoctor: Are the voices you hear ever critical of you or others?\n\nPatient: No, not really critical. They’re more saying which patient records have been changed. It comes through the call-light speaker or the vents, and it sounds very clear, like someone right next to me, even when nobody else hears it.\n\nDoctor: Are the voices you hear something that happens all the time, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go. It’s not every single minute, but when it happens it’s very clear—like through the call-light speaker or the vents, telling me which records were changed. Even if the unit is quiet and nobody else hears anything, I hear it like someone’s right next to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or bothered by the voices you hear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does bother me. Mostly because it sounds so real, like somebody is right there by me, coming through the call-light speaker or the hallway vents. And when the unit is quiet and nobody else hears anything, that’s what upsets me—I’m hearing these voices talking about which patient records were changed, and I don’t know why I’m the only one hearing it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It comes through the call-light speaker or the vents, and it sounds very clear, like someone right next to me, even when nobody else hears it.\n\nDoctor: Are the voices you hear something that happens all the time, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go. It’s not every single minute, but when it happens it’s very clear—like through the call-light speaker or the vents, telling me which records were changed. Mostly because it sounds so real, like somebody is right there by me, coming through the call-light speaker or the hallway vents. It’s more what I hear—the voices coming through the call-light speaker or the vents, talking about records being changed. I don’t think I’ve been seeing anything that other people don’t see.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or have trouble putting your thoughts together when talking to someone?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I usually have trouble saying what I mean, but sometimes I might pause or lose my train of thought. I haven’t really noticed it as a big problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve been upset and worn out, but I don’t know if I’d call it depressed. I haven’t really been thinking about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your mood over time?\n\nPatient: Yes. I don’t keep close track of my sleep. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I might feel more alert, but I haven’t really noticed whether I’m actually sleeping less or needing less sleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel extremely happy or irritable for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes. Some days I feel like I’m finally awake, like I’m supposed to fix everything at work. It can feel really good at first, like I understand what needs to be done. But if my husband or one of the nurses tells me to slow down, I get really irritated and snap at them, like they’re getting in the way of something important.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these high mood days come on suddenly, or do they just gradually happen over time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t notice a clear start to it. Sometimes it feels like I just realize I’m already in that state, if that makes sense. I can’t say whether it comes on suddenly or builds up slowly.\n\nDoctor: On those days when you feel really awake and focused, do you have more energy than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ll finish a full shift and still feel like I have to keep going. I’ll come home and start cleaning, rewriting notes, sorting supplies, and I’ll just pace around the kitchen. My husband will be asking me to please sit down, and I feel like I can’t.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s been kind of ongoing for about two months now, since they asked me to help lead the wound-care documentation rollout. It’s not every single minute, but when it happens it’s very clear—like through the call-light speaker or the vents, telling me which records were changed. Even when it’s quiet and nobody else reacts to it.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever tell you to do something or take a specific action?\n\nPatient: Not really telling me to do something, no. It’s more like they’re telling me which patient records have been changed. I hear it through the call-light speaker or the vents, clear like someone is right there, but other people don’t seem to hear it.\n\nDoctor: Are the voices you hear ever critical of you or others?\n\nPatient: No, not really critical. They’re more saying which patient records have been changed. It comes through the call-light speaker or the vents, and it sounds very clear, like someone right next to me, even when nobody else hears it.\n\nDoctor: Are the voices you hear something that happens all the time, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go. It’s not every single minute, but when it happens it’s very clear—like through the call-light speaker or the vents, telling me which records were changed. Even if the unit is quiet and nobody else hears anything, I hear it like someone’s right next to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or bothered by the voices you hear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does bother me. Mostly because it sounds so real, like somebody is right there by me, coming through the call-light speaker or the hallway vents. And when the unit is quiet and nobody else hears anything, that’s what upsets me—I’m hearing these voices talking about which patient records were changed, and I don’t know why I’m the only one hearing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever see things that other people do not see?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t really see things. It’s more what I hear—the voices coming through the call-light speaker or the vents, talking about records being changed. I don’t think I’ve been seeing anything that other people don’t see.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or have trouble putting your thoughts together when talking to someone?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I usually have trouble saying what I mean, but sometimes I might pause or lose my train of thought. I haven’t really noticed it as a big problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve been upset and worn out, but I don’t know if I’d call it depressed. I haven’t really been thinking about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your mood over time?\n\nPatient: Yes. Some days my mood is way up, like I’m finally awake and I’m supposed to fix everything at work. I get really focused on it. But then if my husband or one of the nurses tells me to slow down, I get irritated fast and snap at them, like they’re getting in the way of something important.\n\nDoctor: When you have those high mood days, do you feel like you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t keep close track of my sleep. On those days I might feel more awake, but I can’t say for sure that I need less sleep than usual.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself talking more than usual on those days when your mood is really high?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. When I start explaining the documentation problem, I can hear myself talking faster and faster. I know I’m going too fast, but I can’t really stop, because if I pause it feels like I’ll lose the whole thread.\n\nDoctor: On those days when you feel really awake and focused, do you need less sleep than you usually do?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I might feel more alert, but I haven’t really noticed whether I’m actually sleeping less or needing less sleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel extremely happy or irritable for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes. Some days I feel like I’m finally awake, like I’m supposed to fix everything at work. It can feel really good at first, like I understand what needs to be done. But if my husband or one of the nurses tells me to slow down, I get really irritated and snap at them, like they’re getting in the way of something important.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these high mood days come on suddenly, or do they just gradually happen over time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t notice a clear start to it. Sometimes it feels like I just realize I’m already in that state, if that makes sense. I can’t say whether it comes on suddenly or builds up slowly.\n\nDoctor: On those days when you feel really awake and focused, do you have more energy than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ll finish a full shift and still feel like I have to keep going. I’ll come home and start cleaning, rewriting notes, sorting supplies, and I’ll just pace around the kitchen. My husband will be asking me to please sit down, and I feel like I can’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you return to a normal mood after these high energy periods?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I really go back to normal, no. It’s been kind of ongoing for about two months now, since they asked me to help lead the wound-care documentation rollout. Since then I feel like I keep getting pulled back into that high-energy mode.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these high-energy periods are separate episodes, or is it more like your mood has just been high for a long time?\n\nPatient: It feels more like it’s been high for a long time. Like I said, it’s been about two months now, since they asked me to help lead that wound-care documentation rollout. It hasn’t really felt like separate little episodes to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have distinct episodes of high energy or if it's been a constant state over the past two months?\n\nPatient: It’s been more constant, I think. Not really separate episodes. It’s been going on about two months, since I was asked to help lead the wound-care documentation rollout.\n\nDoctor: On those days when you have a lot of energy, do you feel like you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t paid close enough attention to whether I’m actually needing less sleep, or if I’m just up and moving because I feel keyed up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F23", "F31.2"], "note": "", "is_final": true}
